2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle C\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle C\hfil$\crcr}}}_{i})\ |\ 1\leq i\leq m\}.

An
interpolation sequent
is defined to be an expression of the form
\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}
such that
\Gamma
is a set of role and equality atoms,
\Phi,\Psi
is a set of inequality atoms,
\Delta,\Sigma
is a multiset of labeled concepts,
\mathcal{G}
is an interpolant, and
a,b\in\{1,2\}
with
a\neq b
. For an interpolation sequent of the aforementioned form, we refer to
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
as the
left partition
and
\Gamma,\Psi\vdash\Sigma
as the
right partition
. Recall that for a concept interpolant
I
of a GCI
C\sqsubseteq D
under
\mathcal{O}
, the ontology
\mathcal{O}
is the union of two ontologies
\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{2}
such that
\mathrm{con}(I)\subseteq\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{1},C)\cap\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{2},D)
(see Definition
3
). The use of
a,b\in\{1,2\}
in an interpolation sequent is to keep track of which partition is associated with which ontology, e.g. in
\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{1}|^{2}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{1}|^{2}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}
the left (right) partition is associated with
\mathcal{O}_{1}
(
\mathcal{O}_{2}
, respectively).

(id^{I}_{1})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta,x:A\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:\neg A,\Sigma\ \|\ \{(\ \vdash x:\neg A)\}

(id^{I}_{2})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:A,x:\neg A,\Sigma\ \|\ \{(\ \vdash x:\top)\}

(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi,x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \{(x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\vdash\ )\}

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}

(O)

\Gamma;\Psi\ {}^{b}|^{a}\ \Phi\vdash\Sigma\ {}^{b}|^{a}\ \Delta\ \|\ \overline{\mathcal{G}}

\Gamma,r(x,y);\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}

(\forall r^{I})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:\forall r.C,\Sigma\ \|\ \forall r.\mathcal{G}

\Gamma,r(x,y_{0}),\ldots,r(x,y_{n});\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi,\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n})\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ y_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,y_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}

({\leqslant}nr^{I})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:{\leqslant}nr.C,\Sigma\ \|\ {\leqslant}{n}r.\mathcal{G}

Figure 3:
Rules in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
. The
(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})
,
(\forall r^{I})
, and
({\leqslant}nr^{I})
rules satisfy the same side conditions as
(id_{\dot{=}})
,
(\forall r)
, and
({\leqslant}nr)
, respectively.

Definition 11

(Interpolant Preserving Rules)
.

Let
(r)
be a rule in the set
\{(s_{\dot{=}}),(\sqcup),(\sqcap),(\exists r),({\geqslant}nr)\}
of the form shown below and assume that the active equalities and/or labeled concepts occur in
\Gamma_{i}
and/or
\Sigma_{i}
, respectively, with the principal formula in
\Sigma
.

\Gamma_{i},\Phi,\Psi\vdash\Delta,\Sigma_{i}\ |\ 1\leq i\leq n

(r)

\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi\vdash\Delta,\Sigma

We define its corresponding
interpolant rule
as follows:

\Gamma_{i};\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma_{i}\ \|\ \mathcal{G}_{i}\ |\ 1\leq i\leq n

(r^{I})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{G}_{n}

We refer to a rule
(s_{\dot{=}}^{I})
,
(\sqcup^{I})
,
(\sqcap^{I})
,
(\exists r^{I})
, or
({\geqslant}nr^{I})
as an
interpolant preserving rule
, or
IP-rule
. We stipulate that
(\exists r^{I})
and
({\geqslant}nr^{I})
are subject to the same side conditions as
(\exists r)
and
({\geqslant}nr)
, respectively, w.r.t. the propagation graph
\mathrm{PG}(\Gamma)
.

For each sequent calculus
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
, we define its corresponding
interpolation calculus
accordingly:

\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O}):=\{(id^{I}_{1}),(id^{I}_{2}),(O)\}\cup\{(r^{I})\ |\ (r)\in\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\setminus(id)\}

Observe that interpolation calculi contain IP-rules as well as rules from Figure
3
. In an interpolation calculus
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
, the
(id^{I}_{1})
,
(id^{I}_{2})
, and
(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})
rules are the
initial rules
,
(O)
is the
orthogonal rule
,
(s_{\dot{=}}^{I})
is the
substitution rule
, and all remaining rules are
logical rules
. The orthogonal rule cuts the number of rules needed in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
roughly in half as it essentially ‘swaps’ the left and right partition permitting rules to be defined that only operate within the right partition; cf.
Lyon et al. (2020)
. A
proof
, its
height
, and the provability relation
\Vdash
are defined in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
in the same manner as for
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
.

We now put forth a sequence of lemmas culminating in the main interpolation theorem (Theorem
3
), which implies that
\mathcal{RIQ}
has the CBP (Corollary
2
). We remark that Lemmas
5
and
7
describe proof transformation algorithms between
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
and
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
. In particular, Lemma
5
states that each proof in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
of a sequent
\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi\vdash\Delta,\Sigma
in a special form can be transformed into a proof in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
of a specific interpolation sequent
\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}
. Then, via Lemma
7
, this proof can be transformed into two proofs in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
witnessing that the interpolant
\mathcal{G}
is ‘implied by’ the left partition
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
and ‘implies’ the right partition
\Gamma,\Psi\vdash\Sigma
. Both Lemmas
5
and
7
are shown by induction on the height of the given proof. Last, when we use the notation
\Gamma,\Phi_{a},\Phi_{b}\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta_{a},\Delta_{b},\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}
or the notation
\Gamma;\Phi_{a}\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Phi_{b}\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta_{a}\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Delta_{b},\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}\ \|\ \mathcal{G}
, we assume that
\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}}:=x_{1}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}},\ldots,x_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}}
such that
\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma,\Phi_{c},\Delta_{c})=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
and
c\in\{a,b\}
. The use of
\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}}
ensures each partition satisfies its respective ontology.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{1}\cup\mathcal{O}_{2}
be an ontology and suppose that
\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}
has a proof
\pi
in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
with
\Phi\cap\Psi=\emptyset
. Then,
\pi
can be transformed into a proof in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
of
\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}\ \|\ \mathcal{G}
such that:

(1)

If
x\,\dot{=}\,y
occurs in
\mathcal{G}
, then
x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\in\Phi
;

(2)

If
x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y
occurs in
\mathcal{G}
, then
x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\in\Psi
;

(3)

\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}})
;

(4)

\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{a},\Delta)\cap\mathrm{con}(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}_{b})
.

The following lemma states that a double orthogonal transformation always ‘preserves’ some of the sequents from the original interpolant. As shown in the appendix, the lemma is helpful in proving Lemma
7
.

Lemma 6
.

If
(\Sigma\vdash\Pi)\in\overline{\overline{\mathcal{G}}}
, then there exists a
(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\Gamma\subseteq\Sigma
and
\Delta\subseteq\Pi
.

Lemma 7
.

If
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}
, then

(1)

For each
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi_{i})\in\mathcal{G}
,
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\Phi\vdash\Delta,\Pi_{i}
;

(2)

For each
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi_{i})\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}
,
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\Psi\vdash\Pi_{i},\Sigma
.

Next, we prove that an interpolant containing at most a single label, i.e. an interpolant of the form

\mathcal{G}:=\{(\ \vdash x:C_{i,1},\ldots,x:C_{i,k_{i}})\ |\ 1\leq i\leq n\}

can be transformed into a single labeled concept within the context of a proof. Toward this end, we define
x:\bigsqcap\bigsqcup\mathcal{G}:=x:\bigsqcap_{1\leq i\leq n}\bigsqcup_{1\leq j\leq k_{i}}C_{i,j}
, where
\mathcal{G}
is as above. The following two lemmas are straightforward and follow by applying the
(\sqcup)
and
(\sqcap)
rules in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
a sufficient number of times.

Lemma 8
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,\Sigma
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
for all
(\ \vdash\Sigma)\in\mathcal{G}
and
\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{G})=\{x\}
, then
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\bigsqcap\bigsqcup\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 9
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,\Sigma
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
for all
(\ \vdash\Sigma)\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{G})=\{x\}
, then
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\dot{\neg}\bigsqcap\bigsqcup\mathcal{G}
.

Our main theorem below is a consequence of Lemmas
4
–
9
. Given a proof of
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:\dot{\neg}C\sqcup D
, we obtain proofs of
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}},x:\dot{\neg}C,x:I
and
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{2}},x:D,x:\dot{\neg}I
in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
by Lemmas
4
,
5
, and
7
–
9
with
I=\bigsqcap\bigsqcup\mathcal{G}
. The concept interpolant
I
is computed in
\mathsf{EXPTIME}
due to the potential use of the
(O)
rule, which may exponentially increase the size of interpolants.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{1}\cup\mathcal{O}_{2}
be a
\mathcal{RIQ}
ontology. If
\mathcal{O}\vDash C\sqsubseteq D
, i.e.
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O}),\pi\Vdash C\sqsubseteq D
, then a concept interpolant
I
can be computed in
\mathsf{EXPTIME}
relative to
s(\pi)
such that
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash C\sqsubseteq I
and
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash I\sqsubseteq D
, i.e.
\mathcal{O}\vDash C\sqsubseteq I
and
\mathcal{O}\vDash I\sqsubseteq D
.

Let
C
be a complex concept,
\mathcal{O}
be a
\mathcal{RIQ}
ontology,
\Theta\subseteq\mathrm{con}(C,\mathcal{O})
, and suppose
C
is implicitly concept-definable from
\Theta
under
\mathcal{O}
. If we want to find the explicit concept-definition of
C
from
\Theta
under
\mathcal{O}
, we utilize the sequent calculus
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
with
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{O}_{\Theta}
. Since
C
is implicitly concept-definable, we know by (1) in Section
2.2
and Corollary
1
that
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})\Vdash C\sqsubseteq C_{\Theta}
. By applying Theorem
3
, we obtain a concept interpolant
I
for
C\sqsubseteq C_{\Theta}
under
\mathcal{O}
, which serves as an explicit concept-definition by Lemma
1
. Therefore, we have a constructive proof of the following corollary.

Corollary 2
.

\mathcal{RIQ}
has the concept interpolation property and the CBP.

5
Concluding Remarks

We have provided novel sequent calculi for
\mathcal{RIQ}
ontologies, showing them sound and complete, as well as showing that each calculus enjoys useful hp-admissibility and hp-invertibility properties. Our sequent systems are modular as the omission of certain rules or constructs from ontologies yields sequent systems for restrictions of
\mathcal{RIQ}
. Moreover, we presented a sequent-based methodology for computing concept interpolants and explicit definitions of implicitly definable concepts using
\mathcal{RIQ}
as a case study, thus satisfying a demand for developing constructive interpolation and definability methods. To the best of our knowledge, we have provided the first proof of the CBP for
\mathcal{RIQ}
.

There are various avenues of future research. First, it would be interesting to know the size and complexity of computing a concept interpolant
I
relative to
C\sqsubseteq D
rather than from a proof
\pi
witnessing
\mathcal{O}\vDash C\sqsubseteq D
. This can be achieved by supplying a proof-search algorithm that generates a proof of
C\sqsubseteq D
, whose relative complexity and size can then be determined. Second, we aim to generalize our methodology to decide and compute the existence of
Craig interpolants
for
\mathcal{RIQ}
and related DLs, which is a non-trivial problem (see
ten Cate et al. (2013)
). Last, we could generalize our method to consider constructs beyond those in
\mathcal{RIQ}
, e.g. negations over roles, intersections of roles, nominals, or the @ operator; it is known that for some of these extensions, e.g. nominals, even concept interpolation fails
Artale et al. (2023)
, requiring an increase in complexity to decide the existence of interpolants.

Appendix A
Proofs for Section
2

Lemma
1
.

If a DL
\mathcal{L}
enjoys the concept interpolation property, then it enjoys the CBP.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an
\mathcal{L}
-ontology,
C
be an
\mathcal{L}
-concept, and
\Theta\subseteq\mathrm{con}(C,\mathcal{O})
. Suppose
\mathcal{L}
enjoys the concept interpolation property and let
C
be implicitly concept-definable from
\Theta
under
\mathcal{O}
, that is,
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{O}_{\Theta}\vDash C\sqsubseteq C_{\Theta}
(see (
1
) on p.
1
). Then, a concept interpolant
I
exists such that (i)
\mathrm{con}(I)\subseteq\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O},C)\cap\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{\Theta},C_{\Theta})
, (ii)
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{O}_{\Theta}\vDash C\sqsubseteq I
, and (iii)
\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{O}_{\Theta}\vDash I\sqsubseteq D
. By (i) and the definitions of
\mathcal{O}_{\Theta}
and
C_{\Theta}
, we have that
\mathrm{con}(I)\subseteq\Theta
. We now establish that
\mathcal{O}\vDash C\equiv I
. We argue that
\mathcal{O}\vDash C\sqsubseteq I
, and note that the argument showing
\mathcal{O}\vDash I\sqsubseteq C
is similar.

Suppose
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
. We aim to show that
\mathcal{I}\vDash C\sqsubseteq I
. First, recall that each concept name
B\in\mathrm{con}(C,\mathcal{O})
such that
B\not\in\Theta
is replaced by a fresh concept name in
C_{\Theta}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\Theta}
, which we denote by
B^{\prime}
. Now, let us define
\mathcal{J}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{J}},\cdot^{\mathcal{J}})
such that
\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}:=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, for each
B\in\mathrm{con}(C,\mathcal{O})
such that
B\not\in\Theta
,
(B^{\prime})^{\mathcal{J}}:={B}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and for all other symbols
P
, let
P^{\mathcal{J}}:={P}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Observe that
\mathcal{J}\vDash\mathcal{O}\cup\mathcal{O}_{\Theta}
, meaning
\mathcal{J}\vDash C\sqsubseteq I
by (ii) above. Furthermore, observe that for
P\in\mathrm{con}(C,\mathcal{O})\cup\mathsf{N_{R}}
,
P^{\mathcal{J}}={P}^{\mathcal{I}}
, meaning
\mathcal{I}\vDash C\sqsubseteq I
as
C
and
I
are only composed of symbols from
\mathrm{con}(C,\mathcal{O})\cup\mathsf{N_{R}}
. It can be argued in a similar fashion that
\mathcal{O}\vDash I\sqsubseteq C
, meaning,
\mathcal{O}\vDash C\equiv I
.

Therefore,
I
serves as an explicit concept-definition of
C
, showing that
C
is explicitly concept-definable from
\Theta
under
\mathcal{O}
, that is,
\mathcal{L}
has the CBP.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for Section
3

Lemma
2
.

Let
\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},{\cdot}^{\mathcal{I}})
be an interpretation,
\mathcal{O}
be a
\mathcal{RIQ}
ontology,
\lambda
be a label assignment, and
\Gamma
be a set of structural atoms. If
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
, and
\mathrm{PG}(\Gamma)\vDash[x]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[y]_{\Gamma}
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r)
, then
(\lambda(x),\lambda(y))\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
, and
[x]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[y]_{\Gamma}
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r)
. We prove the claim by induction on the length of the derivation of the string
S\in L
such that
[x]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{S}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[y]_{\Gamma}
.

Base Case.
For the base case, we suppose that the derivation of
S
is of length
0
. By Definition
5
, we know that the only derivation in
\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})
from
r
of length
0
is the derivation of the form
r
, that is, the derivation consisting solely of
r
. Therefore,
[x]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{r}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[y]_{\Gamma}
, which implies that either
r(z,w)\in\Gamma
or
\mathsf{Inv}(r)(w,z)\in\Gamma
for
z\in[x]_{\Gamma}
and
w\in[y]_{\Gamma}
by Definition
6
. In either case, since
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
, we have that
(\lambda(x),\lambda(y))\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

Inductive Step.
Let the derivation of
S
be of length
n+1
. By Definition
5
, there is a derivation
r\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}RsT
of length
n
and production rule
s\longrightarrow s_{1}\cdots s_{m}\in\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})
such that
S=Rs_{1}\cdots s_{m}T
. As
S
is a string encoding a propagation path in
\mathrm{PG}(\Gamma)
, it follows that
[z_{1}]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{s_{1}}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z_{2}]_{\Gamma}\cdots[z_{m}]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{s_{m}}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z_{m+1}]_{\Gamma}
occurs in
\mathrm{PG}(\Gamma)
. By Definition
6
,
\hat{s}_{1}(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime}),\ldots,\hat{s}_{m}(z_{m}^{\prime},z_{m+1}^{\prime})\in\Gamma
with
z_{i}^{\prime}\in[z_{i}]_{\Gamma}
and

\hat{s}_{i+1}(z_{i}^{\prime},z_{i+1}^{\prime})\in\{s_{i+1}(z_{i}^{\prime},z_{i+1}^{\prime}),\mathsf{Inv}(s_{i+1})(z_{i+1}^{\prime},z_{i}^{\prime})\}

for
1\leq i\leq m
. This implies that
(\lambda(z_{1}),\lambda(z_{m+1}))\in{(s_{1}\circ\cdots\circ s_{m})}^{\mathcal{I}}
because
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
. Furthermore, since
s\longrightarrow s_{1}\cdots s_{m}\in\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})
, either
s_{1}\circ\cdots\circ s_{m}\sqsubseteq s\in\mathcal{O}
or
\mathsf{Inv}(s_{m})\circ\cdots\circ\mathsf{Inv}(s_{1})\sqsubseteq\mathsf{Inv}(s)\in\mathcal{O}
, by Definition
4
. Regardless of the case,
(\lambda(z_{1}),\lambda(z_{m+1}))\in{s}^{\mathcal{I}}
. We will use this fact to complete the proof of the inductive step below.

Let us consider the set
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma,s(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{m+1}^{\prime})
of relational atoms. We know that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma^{\prime}
because
\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
by assumption and
(\lambda(z_{0}),\lambda(z_{m+1}))\in{s}^{\mathcal{I}}
by what was argued above. Moreover, we have that
\mathrm{PG}(\Gamma^{\prime})\vDash[x]_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{R}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z_{1}]_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{s}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z_{m+1}]_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{T}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[y]_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. Observe that
RsT
has a derivation of length
n
by what was said above. Therefore, we may invoke the induction hypothesis, from which it follows that
(\lambda(x),\lambda(y))\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
∎

Theorem
1

(Soundness)
.

If
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma\vdash\Delta
, then
\vDash_{\mathcal{O}}\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.

Proof.

By induction on the height of the proof of
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.

Base Case.
If the height of the proof is
1
, then our proof is an instance of
(id)
or
(id_{\dot{=}})
, as shown below.

(id)

\Gamma\vdash x:A,x:\neg A,\Delta

(id_{\dot{=}})

\Gamma,x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\vdash\Delta

We argue the
(id)
case first, and assume for a contradiction that
\Gamma\vdash x:A,x:\neg A,\Delta
is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
. From this, it follows that there exists an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and a label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:A,x:\neg A,\Delta
. Hence,
\lambda(x)\notin A^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\lambda(x)\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
, which is a contradiction. For the
(id_{\dot{=}})
case, we assume for a contradiction that
\Gamma,x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\vdash\Delta
is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
. Then, there exists an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma,x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}\Delta
. By the side condition
x=^{*}_{\Gamma}y
, we have that
\lambda(x)=\lambda(y)
. However, we also have that
\lambda(x)\neq\lambda(y)
, which is a contradiction.

Inductive Step
. Assume soundness holds for proofs of height
n
. We now show that it holds for proofs of height
n+1
. We prove each case by contraposition and argue that if the conclusion of the last inference of the proof is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
, then at least one premise of the inference must be invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
.

The
(s_{\dot{=}})
rule:

\Gamma\vdash x:L,y:L,\Delta

(s_{\dot{=}})

\Gamma\vdash x:L,\Delta

Suppose there exists an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:L,\Delta
. By the side condition
x=^{*}_{\Gamma}y
imposed on
(s_{\dot{=}})
, we know that
\lambda(x)=\lambda(y)
. Since we have that
\lambda(x)\not\in{L}^{\mathcal{I}}
, it follows that
\lambda(y)\not\in{L}^{\mathcal{I}}
, which shows the premise invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
.

The
(\sqcup)
rule:

\Gamma\vdash x:C,x:D,\Delta

(\sqcup)

\Gamma\vdash x:C\sqcup D,\Delta

Assume
\Gamma\vdash x:C\sqcup D,\Delta
is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
. By Definition
8
, it follows that there exists an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and a label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:C\sqcup D,\Delta
. Hence,
\lambda(x)\notin{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\lambda(x)\notin{D}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Moreover, it holds that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}\Delta
. Since
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:C,x:D,\Delta
, the premise is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
as well.

The
(\sqcap)
rule:

\Gamma\vdash x:C,\Delta

\Gamma\vdash x:D,\Delta

(\sqcap)

\Gamma\vdash x:C\sqcap D,\Delta

Assume
\Gamma\vdash x:C\sqcap D,\Delta
is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
. By Definition
8
, it follows that there exists an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and a label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:C\sqcap D,\Delta
. Either
\lambda(x)\notin{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
or
\lambda(x)\notin{D}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Also,
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}\Delta
. Therefore, either
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:C,\Delta
or
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:D,\Delta
, meaning, at least one of the premises must be invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
as well.

The
(\exists r)
rule:

\Gamma\vdash x:\exists r.C,y:C,\Delta

(\exists r)

\Gamma\vdash x:\exists r.C,\Delta

Suppose there exists an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:\exists r.C,\Delta
. By the side condition imposed on
(\exists r)
, we know that
[x]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[y]_{\Gamma}
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r)
. By Lemma
2
, we know that
(\lambda(x),\lambda(y))\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
. As
\lambda(x)\notin{\exists r.C}^{\mathcal{I}}
, we know that
\lambda(y)\notin{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
, showing that the premise is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
.

The
(\forall r)
rule:

\Gamma,r(x,y)\vdash y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Delta

(\forall r)

\Gamma\vdash x:\forall r.C,\Delta

Suppose,
\Gamma\vdash x:\forall r.C,\Delta
is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
. From this, it follows that there exists an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and a label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:\forall r.C,\Delta
.
Thus, there is at least one domain element
a\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(\lambda(x),a)\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
a\notin{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
.
We now define a new label assignment
\lambda^{\prime}
such that
\lambda^{\prime}(z)=\lambda(z)
if
z\neq y
and
\lambda^{\prime}(y)=a
.
Hence,
\mathcal{I},\lambda^{\prime}\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma,r(x,y)
and
\lambda^{\prime}(y)\notin{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Moreover, let
y_{i}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}=y:\dot{\neg}D_{1},\ldots y:\dot{\neg}D_{k}
. Since
\top\sqsubseteq D_{j}\in\mathcal{O}
for
1\leq j\leq k
, we have that
\mathcal{I},\lambda^{\prime}\not\vDash^{\exists}y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}
as
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
. Therefore, the premise is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
.

The
({\leqslant}nr)
rule, where
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma,\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}),r(x,y_{0}),\ldots,r(x,y_{n})
:

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash y_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,y_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Delta

({\leqslant}nr)

\Gamma\vdash x:{\leqslant}nr.C,\Delta

Assume,
\Gamma\vdash x:{\leqslant}nr.C,\Delta
is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
. From this, it follows that there exists an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and a label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:{\leqslant}nr.C,\Delta
. It follows that
\lambda(x)\in{({\geqslant}(n{+}1)r.C)}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Thus, there are at least
n+1
many distinct elements
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(\lambda(x),a_{i})\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
a_{i}\in{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
. We now define a new label assignment
\lambda^{\prime}
such that
\lambda^{\prime}(z)=\lambda(z)
if
z\neq y_{i}
with
0\leq i\leq n
and
\lambda^{\prime}(y_{i})=a_{i}
. Hence,
\mathcal{I},\lambda^{\prime}\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}),r(x,y_{0}),\ldots,r(x,y_{n})
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda^{\prime}\not\vDash^{\exists}y_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,\Delta
as
\lambda^{\prime}(y_{i})\in{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
for each
0\leq i\leq n
. Moreover, let
y_{i}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}=y_{i}:\dot{\neg}D_{1},\ldots y_{i}:\dot{\neg}D_{k}
for
0\leq i\leq n
. Since
\top\sqsubseteq D_{j}\in\mathcal{O}
for
1\leq j\leq k
, we have that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}y_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}
as
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
. Therefore, the premise is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
.

The
({\geqslant}nr)
rule:

\Gamma\vdash y_{i}:C,x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta\ |\ 1\leq i\leq n

\Gamma,y_{i}\,\dot{=}\,y_{j}\vdash x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta\ |\ 1\leq i<j\leq n

({\geqslant}nr)

\Gamma\vdash x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta

Assume,
\Gamma\vdash x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta
is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
. It follows that there exists an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and a label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma
and
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta
. We note that if
n=0
, then
\lambda(x)\notin{({\geqslant}0r.C)}^{\mathcal{I}}
, which cannot be the case, and hence, we may suppose that
n>0
. Then, we have that
\lambda(x)\in{({\leqslant}(n{-}1)r.C)}^{\mathcal{I}}
. From the side condition on
({\geqslant}nr)
, there are labels
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
such that
[x]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[y_{i}]_{\Gamma}
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r)
, which, by Lemma
2
, implies that
(\lambda(x),\lambda(y_{i}))\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
. If
\lambda(y_{i})\neq\lambda(y_{j})
for each
1\leq i<j\leq n
, then for some
1\leq i\leq n
, we have that
\lambda(y_{i})\notin{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
; otherwise, we have
\lambda(y_{i})=\lambda(y_{j})
for some
1\leq i<j\leq n
. The former cases show that at least one premise in the first set is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
and the latter case shows that at least one premise in the second set of premises is invalid relative to
\mathcal{O}
.
∎

Lemma
3
.

The
(\top)
rule is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
, and the
(\ell^{x}_{y})
,
(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})
,
(w_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
,
(w)
,
(c)
, and
(s_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
rules are hp-admissible.

Proof.

Recall that
\top=A\sqcup\neg A
for some fixed
A\in\mathsf{N_{C}}
. The proof below shows that
(\top)
provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
.

(id)

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:A,x:\neg A

(\sqcup)

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:A\sqcup\neg A

The hp-admissibility of
(\ell^{x}_{y})
is shown by induction on the height of the given proof. The base cases are trivial as any application of
(\ell^{x}_{y})
to an initial rule yields another instance of the initial rule. In the inductive step, with the exception of the
(\forall r)
and
({\leqslant}nr)
cases, all cases are resolved by invoking IH and then applying the rule. In the
(\forall r)
and
({\leqslant}nr)
cases, the label substituted into the sequent may be fresh in the
(\forall r)
or
({\leqslant}nr)
inference, requiring two applications of IH in order for the case to go through. To demonstrate this, we show how a problematic
(\forall r)
case is resolved and note that the
({\leqslant}nr)
case is similar. Suppose we have an instance of
(\forall r)
followed by
(\ell^{y}_{z})
as shown below left, where
y
is fresh in
(\forall r)
. The case is resolved as shown below right, where
(\ell^{v}_{y})
is applied in the first IH application with
v
fresh due to the side condition of the rule,
(\ell^{y}_{z})
is then applied in the second IH application, and last,
(\forall r)
is applied.

\Gamma,r(x,y)\vdash y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Delta

(\forall r)

\Gamma\vdash x:\forall r.C,\Delta

(\ell^{y}_{z})

\Gamma(y/z)\vdash x:\forall r.C,\Delta(y/z)

\Gamma,r(x,y)\vdash y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Delta

IH

\Gamma,r(x,v)\vdash v:C,v:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Delta

IH

\Gamma(y/z),r(x,v)\vdash v:C,v:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Delta(y/z)

(\forall r)

\Gamma(y/z)\vdash x:\forall r.C,\Delta(y/z)

The hp-admissibility of
(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})
,
(w_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
, and
(w)
are shown by induction on the height of the given proof while making a case distinction on the last rule applied. The base cases are trivial as any application of either rule to
(id)
or
(id_{\dot{=}})
yields another instance of the rule, and with the exception of the
({\geqslant}nr)
case, every case of the inductive step may be resolved by applying IH followed by the corresponding rule. The
({\geqslant}nr)
case is trivial when showing the hp-admissibility of
(w_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
and
(w)
, however, an interesting case arises when showing the hp-admissibility of
(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})
. Suppose we have an application of
({\geqslant}nr)
as shown below, which weakens in an equality
y_{i}\,\dot{=}\,y_{j}
that is active in the
({\geqslant}nr)
application. Observe that the desired conclusion is obtained by taking the proof of the premise
\Gamma,y_{i}\,\dot{=}\,y_{j}\vdash x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta
. All other cases where
({\geqslant}nr)
is followed by an application of
(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})
are simple or resolved similarly.

\Gamma\vdash y_{i}:C,x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta\ |\ 1\leq i\leq n

\Gamma,y_{i}\,\dot{=}\,y_{j}\vdash x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta\ |\ 1\leq i<j\leq n

({\geqslant}nr)

\Gamma\vdash x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta

(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})

\Gamma,y_{i}\,\dot{=}\,y_{j}\vdash x:{\geqslant}nr.C,\Delta

The hp-admissibility of
(c)
is also shown by induction on the height of the given proof. We note that the hp-admissibility of
(c)
relies on the hp-admissibility of another contraction rule
(c_{r})
, shown below left, which ‘fuses’ two children nodes in a sequent. The hp-admissibility of
(c)
and
(c_{r})
is shown simultaneously by induction on the height of the given proof, though we focus on the
(c)
case as the
(c_{r})
case is similar. Returning back to the proof that
(c)
is hp-admissible, we note that the base cases are trivial since any application of
(c)
to
(id)
or
(id_{\dot{=}})
yields another instance of the rule. In the inductive step, if neither of the contraction formulae
x:C,x:C
are principal in the conclusion of a rule application
(r)
, as shown in the example below right, then the case is resolved by applying IH (i.e. the
(c)
rule) and then the rule
(r)
.

\Gamma,r(x,y),r(x,z)\vdash\Delta

(c_{r})

\Gamma(y/z),r(x,y)\vdash\Delta(y/z)

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime},x:C,x:C

(r)

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:C,x:C

(c)

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:C

Therefore, let us suppose that one of the contraction formulae
x:C,x:C
is principal. We consider the case where the last rule applied above
(c)
is the
(\forall r)
rule, as shown below left. To resolve the case, we apply the hp-invertibility of
(\forall r)
, followed by the an application of
(c_{r})
which applies
(y/z)
, followed by a sufficient number of applications of IH for
(c)
to contract all of the displayed formulae in the consequent, and finally, an application of
(\forall r)
, as shown below right.

\Gamma,r(x,y)\vdash\Delta,y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:\forall r.C

(\forall r)

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\forall r.C,x:\forall r.C

(c)

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\forall r.C

\Gamma,r(x,y)\vdash\Delta,y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:\forall r.C

Lem.
4

\Gamma,r(x,y),r(x,z)\vdash\Delta,y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},z:C,z:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}

(c_{r})

\Gamma,r(x,y)\vdash\Delta,y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}

IH

\Gamma,r(x,y)\vdash\Delta,y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}

(\forall r)

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\forall r.C

The remaining cases are solved in a similar fashion. Also, note that the proof of Lemma
4
does not rely on the hp-admissibility of
(c)
or
(c_{r})
, and therefore, the above argument is not circular.

Last, we argue the hp-admissibility of
(s_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
by induction on the height of the given proof. The only interesting case is the
(id_{\dot{=}})
case in the base case; the base case for
(id)
is trivial, and the inductive step follows in each case by applying IH followed by the rule. Suppose we have an instance of
(id_{\dot{=}})
as shown below left, where the side condition
x=^{*}_{\Gamma}y
holds. Since
x=^{*}_{\Gamma}y
holds
iff

y=^{*}_{\Gamma}x
by definition, we have that the application of
(id_{\dot{=}})
shown below right is a valid application of
(id_{\dot{=}})
, thus showing
(s_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
hp-admissible in this case.

(id_{\dot{=}})

\Gamma,x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y,y\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,x\vdash\Delta

(s_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})

\Gamma,y\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,x\vdash\Delta

(id_{\dot{=}})

\Gamma,y\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,x\vdash\Delta

The other case when
(s_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
is applied to a non-principal inequality in
(id_{\dot{=}})
is trivial.
∎

Lemma
4
.

All non-initial rules in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
are hp-invertible.

Proof.

The hp-invertibility of
(s_{\dot{=}})
,
(\exists r)
, and
({\geqslant}nr)
follows from the fact that
(w)
(and
(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})
in the
({\geqslant}nr)
case) are hp-admissible (see Lemma
3
above). The remaining cases are shown by induction on the height of the given proof. We only consider the
({\leqslant}nr)
case since all other cases are analogous.

Base case
. Suppose we have instances of
(id)
and
(id_{\dot{=}})
as shown below.

(id)

\Gamma\vdash x:A,x:\neg A,\Delta,z:{\leqslant}nr.C

(id_{\dot{=}})

\Gamma,x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\vdash\Delta,z:{\leqslant}nr.C

The instance of
(id)
shown below top and the instance of
(id_{\dot{=}})
shown below bottom resolve the base case. Note that we take
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma,\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(w_{0},\ldots,w_{n}),r(z,w_{0}),\ldots,r(z,w_{n})
.

(id)

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash x:A,x:\neg A,\Delta,w_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,w_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\ldots,w_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,w_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}

(id_{\dot{=}})

\Gamma^{\prime},x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\vdash\Delta,w_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,w_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\ldots,w_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,w_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}

Inductive Step
. We only consider the
(s_{\dot{=}})
and
({\leqslant}nr)
cases as the remaining cases are similar.

(s_{\dot{=}})
. Suppose we have an instance of
(s_{\dot{=}})
as shown below left. We can resolve the case as shown below right, where
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma,\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(w_{0},\ldots,w_{n}),r(z,w_{0}),\ldots,r(z,w_{n})
.

\Gamma\vdash x:L,y:L,\Delta,z:{\leqslant}nr.C

(s_{\dot{=}})

\Gamma\vdash x:L,\Delta,z:{\leqslant}nr.C

\Gamma\vdash x:L,y:L,\Delta,z:{\leqslant}nr.C

IH

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash x:L,y:L,\Delta,w_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,w_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\ldots,w_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,w_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}

(s_{\dot{=}})

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash x:L,\Delta,w_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,w_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\ldots,w_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,w_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}

({\leqslant}nr)
. There are two cases to consider when the last rule applied is
({\leqslant}nr)
. Either, the principal formula is the formula we want to invert, or it is not. In the first case, shown below, we simply take the proof of the premise, where
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma,\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}),r(x,y_{0}),\ldots,r(x,y_{n})
.

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash y_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,y_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Delta

({\leqslant}nr)

\Gamma\vdash x:{\leqslant}nr.C,\Delta

In the second case, we simply apply IH and then the
({\leqslant}nr)
rule to resolve the case.
∎

Theorem
2

(Completeness)
.

If
\vDash_{\mathcal{O}}\emptyset\vdash x:C
, then
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\emptyset\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:C
.

Proof.

We describe a proof-search algorithm
\mathtt{Prove}
that takes a sequent
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{,}x:C
as input and attempts to construct a proof thereof. We assume that
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{,}x:C
is not provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
and show how to construct an interpretation
\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},{\cdot}^{\mathcal{I}})
and define a label assignment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash_{\mathcal{O}}\emptyset\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{,}x:C
, meaning
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
and
\lambda(x)\not\in{C}^{\mathcal{I}}
, that is,
\not\vDash_{\mathcal{O}}\emptyset\vdash x:C
. Let us now begin our description of
\mathtt{Prove}
.

\mathtt{Prove}
. We take
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:C
as input and move to the step below.

(id)
and
(id_{\dot{=}})
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
be all branches of the pseudo-proof
\pi
currently under construction. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each branch, respectively. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, if
S_{i}
is an instance of
(id)
or
(id_{\dot{=}})
, then apply
(id)
and
(id_{\dot{=}})
, respectively, bottom-up on
\mathcal{B}_{i}
and close that branch, i.e. halt
\mathtt{Prove}
on the branch
\mathcal{B}_{i}
. If
\mathtt{Prove}
has halted on every branch, then return
\mathtt{True}
. Otherwise, if a sequent
S_{i}
exists such that no rule from
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
is bottom-up applicable to it, copy it above itself and continue to step
(s_{\dot{=}})
below.

(s_{\dot{=}})
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
be all branches of the pseudo-proof
\pi
currently under construction. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each branch, respectively. We consider each branch and its top sequent in turn. Let us suppose we have already considered
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{i}
so that
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
is currently under consideration. Let the top sequent
S_{i+1}
be of the form:

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x_{1}:L_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:L_{k}

with all labeled literals displayed. For each
1\leq i\leq k
and each
y\in\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma)
such that
x_{i}=^{*}_{\Gamma}y
, repeatedly apply the
(s_{\dot{=}})
rule bottom-up, extending
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
. After all branches have been processed in this way, we move onto the
(\sqcup)
case below.

(\sqcup)
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
be all branches of the pseudo-proof
\pi
currently under construction. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each branch, respectively. We consider each branch and its top sequent in turn. Let us suppose we have already considered
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{i}
so that
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
is currently under consideration. Let the top sequent
S_{i+1}
be of the form:

\Gamma\vdash x_{1}:C_{1}\sqcup D_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:C_{k}\sqcup D_{k},\Delta

with all disjunctive formulae displayed. We repeatedly apply the
(\sqcup)
rule bottom-up, extending
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
so that it now has a top sequent of the form:

\Gamma\vdash x_{1}:C_{1},x_{1}:D_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:C_{k},x_{k}:D_{k},\Delta

After all branches have been processed in this way, we move onto the
(\sqcap)
case below.

(\sqcap)
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
be all branches of the pseudo-proof
\pi
currently under construction. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each branch, respectively. We consider each branch and its top sequent in turn. Let us suppose we have already considered
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{i}
so that
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
is currently under consideration. Let the top sequent
S_{i+1}
be of the form:

\Gamma\vdash x_{1}:C_{1}\sqcap D_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:C_{k}\sqcap D_{k},\Delta

with all conjunctive formulae displayed. We repeatedly apply the
(\sqcap)
rule bottom-up, extending
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
with
2^{k}
new branches with each having a top sequent of the form:

\Gamma\vdash x_{1}:E_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:E_{k},\Delta

where
E_{j}\in\{C_{j},D_{j}\}
for
1\leq j\leq k
. After all branches have been processed in this way, we move onto the
(\exists r)
case below.

(\exists r)
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
be all branches of the pseudo-proof
\pi
currently under construction. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each branch, respectively. We consider each branch and its top sequent in turn. Let us suppose we have already considered
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{i}
so that
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
is currently under consideration. Let the top sequent
S_{i+1}
be of the form:

\Gamma\vdash x_{1}:\exists r_{1}.C_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:\exists r_{k}.C_{k},\Delta

with all existential formulae displayed. For each
1\leq j\leq k
and every label
y\in\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma)
such that
[x_{j}]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[y]_{\Gamma}
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r_{j})
, repeatedly apply the
(\exists r)
rule bottom-up, extending
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
. After all branches have been processed in this way, we move onto the
(\forall r)
case below.

(\forall r)
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
be all branches of the pseudo-proof
\pi
currently under construction. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each branch, respectively. We consider each branch and its top sequent in turn. Let us suppose we have already considered
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{i}
so that
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
is currently under consideration. Let the top sequent
S_{i+1}
be of the form:

\Gamma\vdash x_{1}:\forall r_{1}.C_{1},\ldots,x_{k}:\forall r_{k}.C_{k},\Delta

with all universal formulae displayed. For each
1\leq j\leq k
, repeatedly apply the
(\forall r)
rule bottom-up, extending
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
, and so the top sequent contains
r_{j}(x_{j},y)
in the antecedent and
y:C_{j},y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}
in the consequent, where
y
is fresh for each
j
. After all branches have been processed in this way, we move onto the
({\leqslant}nr)
case below.

({\leqslant}nr)
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
be all branches of the pseudo-proof
\pi
currently under construction. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each branch, respectively. We consider each branch and its top sequent in turn. Let us suppose we have already considered
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{i}
so that
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
is currently under consideration. Let the top sequent
S_{i+1}
be of the form:

\Gamma\vdash x_{1}:(\leqslant n_{1}r_{1}.C_{1}),\ldots,x_{k}:(\leqslant n_{k}r_{k}.C_{k}),\Delta

with all qualified number restrictions of the form
x_{j}:(\leqslant n_{j}r_{j}.C_{j})
displayed and where
1\leq j\leq k
. We repeatedly apply the
({\leqslant}nr)
rule bottom-up, extending
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
so that the top sequent contains
\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n_{j}})
and
r(x_{j},y_{0}),\ldots,r(x_{j},y_{n_{j}})
in the antecedent and
y_{0}:\dot{\neg}C_{j},y_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\ldots,y_{n_{j}}:\dot{\neg}C_{j},y_{n_{j}}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}
in the consequent with
y_{0},\ldots,y_{n_{j}}
fresh for each considered qualified number restriction of the above form. After all branches have been processed in this way, we move onto the
({\geqslant}nr)
case below.

({\geqslant}nr)
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{n}
be all branches of the pseudo-proof
\pi
currently under construction. Let
S_{1},\ldots,S_{n}
be the top sequents of each branch, respectively. We consider each branch and its top sequent in turn. Let us suppose we have already considered
\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{i}
so that
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
is currently under consideration. Let the top sequent
S_{i+1}
be of the form:

\Gamma\vdash x_{1}:(\geqslant n_{1}r_{1}.C_{1}),\ldots,x_{k}:(\geqslant n_{k}r_{k}.C_{k}),\Delta

with all qualified number restrictions of the form
x_{j}:(\geqslant n_{j}r_{j}.C_{j})
displayed and where
1\leq j\leq k
. For each collection
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n_{j}}\}\subseteq\mathrm{Lab}(S_{i+1})
of labels such that
[x_{j}]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[y_{t}]_{\Gamma}
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r_{j})
and
1\leq t\leq n_{j}
, we apply the
({\geqslant}nr)
rule bottom-up, extending
\mathcal{B}_{i+1}
. After all branches have been processed in this way, we cycle back to the
(id)
and
(id_{\dot{=}})
case.

This concludes the description of
\mathtt{Prove}
.

We know that
\mathtt{Prove}
cannot return
\mathtt{True}
since then a proof of
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:C
would exist, contrary to our assumption. Therefore,
\mathtt{Prove}
will not terminate, meaning that it constructs an infinite pseudo-proof
\pi
in the form of an infinite tree. Since only finite branches occurs within this pseudo-proof, by König’s lemma we know that an infinite branch of the following form exists in
\pi
:

\mathcal{B}=(\Gamma_{0}\vdash\Delta_{0}),(\Gamma_{1}\vdash\Delta_{1}),\ldots,(\Gamma_{n}\vdash\Delta_{n}),\ldots

such that
\Gamma_{0}=\emptyset
and
\Delta_{0}=x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:C
. Let us define
\Gamma^{*}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\Gamma_{i}
and
\Delta^{*}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\Delta_{i}
. We use
\mathcal{B}
to construct an interpretation
\mathcal{I}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}},{\cdot}^{\mathcal{I}})
and define a label assigment
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma^{*}
, but
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}\Delta^{*}
, meaning
\not\vDash_{\mathcal{O}}\emptyset\vdash x:C
. We define
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}=\{[y]\ |\ y\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{B})\}
, where we use
[y]=[y]_{\Gamma^{*}}
for simplicity. In other words,
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
contains all equivalence classes modulo the
=^{*}_{\Gamma^{*}}
relation on labels occurring in the sequents of the branch
\mathcal{B}
. We define
\lambda
and
{\cdot}^{\mathcal{I}}
accordingly:

•

{y}^{\mathcal{I}}=[y]
for
y\in\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{B})
;

•

[y]\in{A}^{\mathcal{I}}

iff

y:\neg A\in\Delta^{*}
;

•

([y],[z])\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}

iff
either (1) there exist
w\in[y]
and
u\in[z]
such that
r(w,u)\in\Gamma^{*}
, or (2)
r_{1}\circ\cdots\circ r_{n}\sqsubseteq r\in\mathcal{O}
and
([x]_{i-1},[x]_{i})\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
with
y\in[x_{0}]
and
z\in[x_{n}]
.

Based on the definition above and the definition of an
\mathbf{R}
-system
\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})
, we note that if
([y],[z])\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
, then there exist roles
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
and
[z_{1}],\ldots,[z_{n-1}]
such that

\mathrm{PG}(\Gamma^{*})\vDash[y]\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{r_{1}}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z_{1}]\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{r_{2}}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt\cdots[z_{n-1}]\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{r_{n}}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z]

and
r_{1}\cdots r_{n}\in L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r)
. It is straightforward to show that
\mathcal{I}
satisfies all RIAs
r_{1}\circ\cdots\circ r_{n}\sqsubseteq r
in
\mathcal{O}
. We now argue that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\vDash^{\forall}\Gamma^{*}
. If
r(y,z)\in\Gamma^{*}
, then
([y],[z])\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
holds by the definition above, showing that
(\lambda(y),\lambda(z))\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
. If
y\,\dot{=}\,z\in\Gamma^{*}
, then by definition
[y]=[z]
, showing
\lambda(y)=\lambda(z)
. Also, observe that if
y\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,z\in\Gamma^{*}
, then it cannot be the case that
[y]=[z]
since then the
(id_{\dot{=}})
rule would be applied in
\mathcal{B}
, implying that
\mathcal{B}
is finite, which is a contradiction as
\mathcal{B}
is infinite; hence,
\lambda(y)\neq\lambda(z)
.
We will now show that
\mathcal{I},\lambda\not\vDash^{\exists}\Delta^{*}
, and afterward, we will argue that
\mathcal{I}
satisfies all GCIs in
\mathcal{O}
, thus establishing that
\mathcal{I}\vDash\mathcal{O}
. We argue by induction on the complexity of
D
that if
y:D\in\Delta^{*}
, then
\lambda(y)\not\in{D}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

y:A\in\Delta^{*}.

If
y:A\in\Delta^{*}
, then for every
z\in[y]
,
z:\neg A\not\in\Delta^{*}
since otherwise
(s_{\dot{=}})
would have been applied, eventually followed by
(id)
and
\mathcal{B}
would be finite, contrary to our assumption. Therefore,
y:\neg A\not\in\Delta^{*}
, implying that
\lambda(y)\notin{A}^{\mathcal{I}}
by the definition of
\mathcal{I}
.

y:\neg A\in\Delta^{*}.

Then,
\lambda(y)\in{A}^{\mathcal{I}}
, by the definition of
\mathcal{I}
.

y:E\sqcup F\in\Delta^{*}.

Then, eventually the
(\sqcup)
rule will be applied in
\mathtt{Prove}
, meaning
y:E,y:F\in\Delta^{*}
, showing that
\lambda(y)\in{E}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\lambda(y)\in{F}^{\mathcal{I}}
by IH. Therefore,
\lambda(y)\in{(E\sqcup F)}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

y:E\sqcap F\in\Delta^{*}.

If
y:E\sqcap F\in\Delta
, then eventually the
(\sqcap)
rule will be applied in
\mathtt{Prove}
, meaning either
y:E\in\Delta
or
y:D\in\Delta
. Hence, either
\lambda(y)\in{E}^{\mathcal{I}}
or
\lambda(y)\in{F}^{\mathcal{I}}
by IH, implying
\lambda(y)\in{(E\sqcap F)}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

y:\exists r.E\in\Delta^{*}.

Suppose
(\lambda(y),\lambda(z))\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.
([y],[z])\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
. It follows that
[y]\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z]
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r)
, meaning at some step in
\mathcal{B}
, we have that
[y]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z]_{\Gamma}
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r)
. Hence, the
(\exists r)
rule will be bottom-up applied, ensuring that
z:E\in\Delta^{*}
. By IH, we have that
\lambda(z)\notin{E}^{\mathcal{I}}
, so since
z
was assumed arbitrary, we have that
\lambda(y)\notin{(\exists r.E)}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

y:\forall r.E\in\Delta^{*}.

If
y:\forall r.E\in\Delta^{*}
, then eventually the
(\forall r)
rule will be bottom-up applied in
\mathtt{Prove}
, ensuring that for some label
z
,
r(y,z)\in\Gamma^{*}
and
z:E\in\Delta^{*}
. By the definition of
{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
,
([y],[z])\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and by IH
\lambda(z)\notin{E}^{\mathcal{I}}
, thus
\lambda(y)\notin{\forall r.E}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

y:{\leqslant}nr.E\in\Delta^{*}.

If
y:{\leqslant}nr.E\in\Delta
, then eventually the
({\leqslant}nr)
rule will be applied bottom-up in
\mathtt{Prove}
. It follows that
\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(z_{0},\ldots,z_{n}),r(y,z_{0}),\ldots,r(y,z_{n})\in\Gamma^{*}
and
y_{0}:\dot{\neg}E,\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}E\in\Delta^{*}
. Therefore, there exist at least
n+1
distinct elements in
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, namely
[y_{0}],\ldots,[y_{n}]
, such that for
0\leq i\leq n
,
([y],[z_{i}])\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
and where
\lambda(z_{i})\notin{E_{i}}^{\mathcal{I}}
by IH. Hence,
\lambda(y)\notin{({\leqslant}nr.E)}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

y:{\geqslant}nr.E\in\Delta^{*}.

Assume that distinct
[z_{1}],\ldots,[z_{n}]\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
exist such that for
1\leq i\leq n
,
([y],[z_{i}])\in{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
. It follows that for
1\leq i\leq n
,
[y]\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z_{i}]
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r)
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
pairwise, distinct labels. Hence, at some step in
\mathcal{B}
, we have that
[y]_{\Gamma}\hskip 3.0pt\raise-3.0pt\hbox{$\overset{\mathclap{L}}{\leadsto}$}\hskip 3.0pt[z_{i}]_{\Gamma}
with
L=L_{\mathrm{G}(\mathcal{O})}(r)
, and so, the
({\geqslant}nr)
rule will be applied bottom-up, ensuring that
z_{i}:E\in\Delta^{*}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
. By IH,
\lambda(z_{i})\notin{E}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Since the elements
[z_{1}],\ldots,[z_{n}]\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
were assumed to be arbitrary and distinct, this shows that for any
n
elements that
\lambda(y)
relates to via
{r}^{\mathcal{I}}
, at least one must not be an element of
{E}^{\mathcal{I}}
, i.e.
\lambda(y)\notin{({\geqslant}nr.E)}^{\mathcal{I}}
.

Let us now argue that
\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{O}
by arguing that all GCIs in
\mathcal{O}
are satisfied on
\mathcal{I}
(note that all RIAs are satisfied on
\mathcal{I}
as stated above). Observe that our input is of the form
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:C
and every time a fresh label is added by the
(\forall r)
or
({\leqslant}nr)
step of
\mathtt{Prove}
, the concepts in
\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}}
are introduced at that label, meaning such concepts will occur at every label in
\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma^{*},\Delta^{*})
. Therefore, by the argument above, we know that for every GCI
\top\sqsubseteq E\in\mathcal{O}
and
\lambda(y)=[y]\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
,
\lambda(y)\notin{\dot{\neg}E}^{\mathcal{I}}
, showing that
\lambda(y)\in{E}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and hence, every GCI is satisfied on
\mathcal{I}
. As
x:C\in\Delta^{*}
, all of the above implies that
\vDash_{\mathcal{O}}\emptyset\vdash x:C
, completing the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Section
4

Lemma
5
.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{1}\cup\mathcal{O}_{2}
be an ontology and suppose that
\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}
has a proof
\pi
in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
with
\Phi\cap\Psi=\emptyset
. Then,
\pi
can be transformed into a proof in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
of
\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}\ \|\ \mathcal{G}
such that:

(1)

If
x\,\dot{=}\,y
occurs in
\mathcal{G}
, then
x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\in\Phi
;

(2)

If
x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y
occurs in
\mathcal{G}
, then
x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\in\Psi
;

(3)

\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}})
;

(4)

\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{a},\Delta)\cap\mathrm{con}(\Sigma,\mathcal{O}_{b})
.

Proof.

By induction on the height of the proof. The base cases are trivial, so we focus on the inductive step. We argue one of the
({\leqslant}nr)
cases as the remaining cases are simple or similar. Suppose we have a proof in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
ending with an application of
({\leqslant}nr)
, as shown below, where
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma,r(x,y_{0}),\ldots,r(x,y_{n})
and let
c\in\{a,b\}
.

\Gamma^{\prime},\Phi,\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}),\Psi\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta,y_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}

({\leqslant}nr)

\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}}^{\prime},\Delta,x:{\leqslant}nr.C,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}^{\prime}

By IH, the premise of the inference shown below has a proof in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
, where
\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}}=\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}}^{\prime},y_{0}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}},\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}}
for
c\in\{a,b\}
. Since properties (1) and (2) hold for the premise below, and
\Phi\cap(\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}),\Psi)=\emptyset
by assumption, we know
y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}\not\in\mathrm{Lab}(\Phi,\Psi)
since otherwise the side condition of the
({\leqslant}nr)
application above would be violated. Hence,
\mathcal{G}
will be in the form dictated by Definition
10
, meaning we obtain a proof of the desired conclusion in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
by a single application of
({\leqslant}nr)
, as shown below.

\Gamma^{\prime};\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}),\Psi\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}},\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ y_{0}:\dot{\neg}C,\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}C,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}\ \|\ \mathcal{G}

({\leqslant}nr)

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}}^{\prime},\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:{\leqslant}nr.C,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}^{\prime}\ \|\ {\leqslant}{n}r.\mathcal{G}

\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}}^{\prime},\Delta,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}^{\prime}){\cup}\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}\}
and
\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{G}){\subseteq}\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{a},\Delta){\cap}\mathrm{con}(y_{0}{:}\dot{\neg}C_{0},\ldots,y_{n}{:}\dot{\neg}C_{n},\Sigma,\mathcal{O}_{b})
follow from IH with the latter also following from the fact that
\mathrm{con}(\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}})\subseteq\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{c})
. (1) and (2) easily hold, and as shown below, property (3) follows from the former fact:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lab}({\leqslant}{n}r.\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{G})\setminus\{y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}}^{\prime},\Delta,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{a}}^{\prime},\Delta,x:{\leqslant}{n}r.C,\Sigma,\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{b}}^{\prime})

while property (4) follows from the latter fact:

\displaystyle\mathrm{con}({\leqslant}{n}r.\mathcal{G})=\ \mathrm{con}(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{a},\Delta)\cap\mathrm{con}(y_{0}:\dot{\neg}C_{0},\ldots,y_{n}:\dot{\neg}C_{n},\Sigma,\mathcal{O}_{b})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{a},\Delta)\cap\mathrm{con}(x:{\leqslant}nr.C,\Sigma,\mathcal{O}_{b})\qed

Lemma
6
.

If
(\Sigma\vdash\Pi)\in\overline{\overline{\mathcal{G}}}
, then there exists a
(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\Gamma\subseteq\Sigma
and
\Delta\subseteq\Pi
.

Proof.

Let
(\Sigma\vdash\Pi)\in\overline{\overline{\mathcal{G}}}
and suppose for a contradiction that no
(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\in\mathcal{G}
exists such that
\Gamma\subseteq\Sigma
and
\Delta\subseteq\Pi
. Then, for each
(\Gamma\vdash\Delta)\in\mathcal{G}
, either
\Gamma\not\subseteq\Sigma
or
\Delta\not\subseteq\Pi
. Let
\mathcal{G}=\{(\Gamma_{1}\vdash\Delta_{1}),\ldots,(\Gamma_{n}\vdash\Delta_{n})\}
and define
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}
such that for each
1\leq i\leq n
, one and only one of the following holds: (1)
x\,\dot{=}\,y\in\Gamma^{\prime}
and
x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\in\Gamma_{i}\setminus\Sigma
, (2)
x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\in\Gamma^{\prime}
and
x\,\dot{=}\,y\in\Gamma_{i}\setminus\Sigma
, or (3)
x:\dot{\neg}C\in\Delta^{\prime}
and
x:C\in\Delta_{i}\setminus\Pi
. Observe that
\Sigma\cap\Gamma^{\prime}=\emptyset
and
\Pi\cap\Delta^{\prime}=\emptyset
by construction. However, since
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}
by Definition
9
, we have that either
\Sigma\cap\Gamma^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
or
\Pi\cap\Delta^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, which gives a contradiction and proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma
7
.

If
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}
, then

1.

For each
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi_{i})\in\mathcal{G}
,
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\Phi\vdash\Delta,\Pi_{i}
;

2.

For each
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi_{i})\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}
,
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\Psi\vdash\Pi_{i},\Sigma
.

Proof.

We prove both claims simultaneously by induction on the height of the proof and make a case distinction on the last rule applied.

Base case.
Suppose we have a proof of height
1
, i.e. an instance of an initial rule. Let us first consider the
(id^{I}_{2})
case and then we will consider the
(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})
case, noting that the
(id^{I}_{1})
case is similar.

(id^{I}_{2})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:A,x:\neg A,\Sigma\ \|\ \{(\ \vdash x:\top)\}

Claim 1 is resolved as shown below left, and relies on Lemma
3
, whereas claim 2 is resolved by applying the
(id)
rule. Note that we use the interpolant
\overline{\mathcal{G}}=\{(\ \vdash x:\bot)\}
in claim 2 (see Definition
9
above) and
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma,x:A,x:\neg A
.

(\top)

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta,x:\top

(id)

\Gamma,\Psi\vdash\Sigma^{\prime},x:\bot

For the
(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})
case, suppose we have a proof consisting of a single application of the
(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})
rule, as shown below.

(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi,x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \{(x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\vdash\ )\}

By the side condition on the rule, we know that
x=^{*}_{\Gamma}y
holds for
(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})
above. Therefore, the application of
(id_{\dot{=}})
shown below left is warranted as is the application of
(id_{\dot{=}})
shown below right, thus resolving the case.

(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})

\Gamma,\Phi,x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y\vdash\Delta

(id_{\dot{=}}^{I})

\Gamma,\Psi,x\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y,x\,\dot{=}\,y\vdash\Sigma

Inductive step.
For the inductive step, we consider the
(O)
,
(\sqcap^{I})
,
(\forall r^{I})
, and
({\leqslant}nr^{I})
cases as the remaining cases are similar.

(O)
. Let our proof in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
end with an
(O)
application:

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}

(O)

\Gamma;\Psi\ {}^{b}|^{a}\ \Phi\vdash\Sigma\ {}^{b}|^{a}\ \Delta\ \|\ \overline{\mathcal{G}}

Observe that if
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi_{i})\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}
, then
\Gamma,\Psi,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Sigma,\Pi_{i}
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
by IH, which demonstrates claim (1). To prove claim (2), let
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi_{i})\in\overline{\overline{\mathcal{G}}}
. Then, by Lemma
6
, there exists a
(\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Pi_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq\Pi_{i}
. By IH,
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma,\Phi,\Sigma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi_{i}^{\prime},\Delta
, implying
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma,\Phi,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi_{i},\Delta
by the hp-admissibility of
(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})
,
(w_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
, and
(w)
(Lemma
3
).

(\sqcap^{I})
. Suppose we have a proof in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
ending with an application of
(\sqcap^{I})
, as shown below.

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:C,\Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}_{1}

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:D,\Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}_{2}

(\sqcap^{I})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:C\sqcup D,\Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}_{1}\cup\mathcal{G}_{2}

We prove claim (1) first. Let
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi)\in\mathcal{G}_{1}\cup\mathcal{G}_{2}
. Regardless of if
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi)\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
or
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi)\in\mathcal{G}_{2}
, we have a proof of
\Gamma,\Phi,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Delta,\Pi
by IH, which proves the claim. To prove claim (2), suppose that
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\vdash\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2})\in\overline{\mathcal{G}_{1}\cup\mathcal{G}_{2}}
such that
(\Gamma_{1}\vdash\Pi_{1})\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{1}
and
(\Gamma_{2}\vdash\Pi_{2})\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
. By IH, the top two sequents below are provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
. We then apply the hp-admissibility of
(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})
,
(w_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
, and
(w)
(Lemma
3
) a sufficient number of times (indicated by the asterisk) to ensure the contexts match. Last, we apply the
(\sqcap)
to obtain the desired conclusion.

\Gamma,\Gamma_{1},\Psi\vdash x:C,\Sigma,\Pi_{1}

(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})^{*},(w_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})^{*},(w)^{*}

\Gamma,\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Psi\vdash x:C,\Sigma,\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}

\Gamma,\Gamma_{2},\Psi\vdash x:D,\Sigma,\Pi_{2}

(w_{\,\dot{=}\,})^{*},(w_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})^{*},(w)^{*}

\Gamma,\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Psi\vdash x:D,\Sigma,\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}

(\sqcap)

\Gamma,\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\Psi\vdash x:C\sqcup D,\Sigma,\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}

(\forall r^{I})
. Suppose our proof ends with an
(\forall r^{I})
application:

\Gamma,r(x,y);\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Sigma\ \|\ \mathcal{G}

(\forall r^{I})

\Gamma;\Phi\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ \Psi\vdash\Delta\ {}^{a}|^{b}\ x:\forall r.C,\Sigma\ \|\ \forall r.\mathcal{G}

We first argue claim (1). Let
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi,y:C_{1},\ldots,y:C_{n})\in\mathcal{G}
such that all concepts labeled with
y
are displayed and
y\not\in\mathrm{Lab}(\Gamma^{\prime})
by assumption, and let
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern . By IH, we obtain a proof in

ending as shown below:

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},r(x,y)\vdash\Delta,\Pi,y:C_{1},\ldots,y:C_{n}

(\sqcup)\times(n{-}1)

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},r(x,y)\vdash\Delta,\Pi,y:\bigsqcup\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern (\forall r)

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Delta,\Pi,x:\forall r.\bigsqcup\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern We now argue claim (2). Observe that for any
(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Pi)\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}
, the multiset

contains zero or more labeled concepts of the form

. We suppose
\Pi
contains one such formula as the other cases are analogous, and let
\Pi=\Pi^{\prime},x:\exists r.(\dot{\neg}C_{1}\sqcap\cdots\sqcap\dot{\neg}C_{n})
. The case is resolved as shown below, where
(\sqcap)^{*}
denotes
n{-}1
applications of
(\sqcap)
between the

premises obtained from IH and
\Sigma^{\prime}=y:C,y:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},\Sigma
.

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},r(x,y)\vdash\Sigma^{\prime},\Pi^{\prime},y:\dot{\neg}C_{i}\ |\ 1\leq i\leq n

(\sqcap)^{*}

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},r(x,y)\vdash\Sigma^{\prime},\Pi^{\prime},y:\bigsqcap\dot{\neg}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern with

fresh to obtain the sequent
S_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}

y_{i}
and
y_{\ell}
have been ‘swapped’. Observe that some inequalities of the form
y_{t}\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y_{s}
in
\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n})
may have ‘flipped’ to
y_{s}\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,y_{t}
, yielding the set
\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}_{i,\ell}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n})
. By applying
(w_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
a sufficient number of times, one can weaken in all inequalities from
\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n})
not occurring in
\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}_{i,\ell}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n})
, and then apply
(s_{\,{\not}{\dot{=}}\,})
a sufficient number of times so that only
\Gamma^{\not\dot{=}}(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n})
occurs in the antecedent.

.

Lemma
8
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,\Sigma
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
for all
(\ \vdash\Sigma)\in\mathcal{G}
and
\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{G})=\{x\}
, then
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\bigsqcap\bigsqcup\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}:=\{(\ \vdash x:C_{i,1},\ldots,x:C_{i,k_{i}})\ |\ 1\leq i\leq n\}
and suppose that
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:C_{i,1},\ldots,x:C_{i,k_{i}}
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
for
1\leq i\leq n
. By repeated application of the
(\sqcup)
rule, we obtain
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\bigsqcup_{1\leq j\leq k_{i}}C_{i,j}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
. Applying the
(\sqcap)
rule
n{-}1
times lets us prove
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\bigsqcap_{1\leq i\leq n}\bigsqcup_{1\leq j\leq k_{i}}C_{i,j}
, which gives us the desired conclusion. ∎

Lemma
9
.

If
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,\Sigma
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
for all
(\ \vdash\Sigma)\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{G})=\{x\}
, then
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\dot{\neg}\bigsqcap\bigsqcup\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\{(\ \vdash\Sigma_{1}),\ldots,(\ \vdash\Sigma_{n})\}
such that
\Sigma_{i}=\{x:C_{i,1},\ldots,x:C_{i,k_{i}}\}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
. We show
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\dot{\neg}\bigsqcap\bigsqcup\mathcal{G}
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
by induction on the cardinality of
\mathcal{G}
.

Base case
. Let us suppose that
\mathcal{G}
is a singleton. Then, by assumption
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\neg C_{1,j}
is provable for all
1\leq j\leq k_{1}
, and so, the conclusion follows by
k_{1}{-}1
applications of
(\sqcap)
.

Inductive Step
. Suppose that
\mathcal{G}=\{(\ \vdash\Sigma_{1}),\ldots,(\ \vdash\Sigma_{n+1})\}
contains
n+1
elements and assume that
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,\Pi
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
for all
\Pi\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}
. Then, for each
1\leq j\leq k_{n+1}
, the sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\dot{\neg}C_{n+1,j},\Sigma^{\prime}
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
for each
(\ \vdash\Sigma^{\prime})\in\overline{(\mathcal{G}\setminus\{(\ \vdash\Sigma_{n+1})\})}
. By IH, for each
1\leq j\leq k_{n+1}
, the following sequent is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
.

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\bigsqcup\bigsqcap\dot{\neg}(\mathcal{G}\setminus\{(\ \vdash\Sigma_{n+1})\}),x:\dot{\neg}C_{n+1,j}

By applying the
(\sqcap)
rule
k_{n+1}{-}1
times between each of the sequents above, followed by a single application of the
(\sqcup)
rule, we obtain a proof of

\Gamma\vdash\Delta,x:\bigsqcup\bigsqcap\dot{\neg}(\mathcal{G}\setminus\{(\ \vdash\Sigma_{n+1})\})\sqcup\!\!\!\!\bigsqcap_{1\leq j\leq k_{n+1}}\!\!\!\!\dot{\neg}C_{n+1,j},

which gives our desired conclusion because

\dot{\neg}\bigsqcap\bigsqcup\mathcal{G}=\bigsqcup\bigsqcap\dot{\neg}(\mathcal{G}\setminus\{(\ \vdash\Sigma_{n+1})\})\sqcup\!\!\!\!\bigsqcap_{1\leq j\leq k_{n+1}}\!\!\!\!\dot{\neg}C_{n+1,j}.\qed

Theorem
3
.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{1}\cup\mathcal{O}_{2}
be a
\mathcal{RIQ}
ontology. If
\mathcal{O}\vDash C\sqsubseteq D
, i.e.
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O}),\pi\Vdash C\sqsubseteq D
, then a concept interpolant
I
can be computed in
\mathsf{EXPTIME}
relative to
s(\pi)
such that
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash C\sqsubseteq I
and
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})\Vdash I\sqsubseteq D
, i.e.
\mathcal{O}\vDash C\sqsubseteq I
and
\mathcal{O}\vDash I\sqsubseteq D
.

Proof.

By our assumption and Corollary
1
, it follows that
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O}),\pi\Vdash C\sqsubseteq D
, meaning
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:\dot{\neg}C\sqcup D
is the conclusion of
\pi
by definition. By Lemma
4
, namely, the hp-invertibility of the
(\sqcup)
rule,
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}},x:\dot{\neg}C,x:D
is provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
with a proof
\pi^{\prime}
. By Lemma
5
, the interpolation sequent
\emptyset;\emptyset\ {}^{1}|^{2}\ \emptyset\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}},x:\dot{\neg}C\ {}^{1}|^{2}\ x:D,x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}\ \|\ \mathcal{G}
is provable in
\mathsf{SI}(\mathcal{O})
with
\mathrm{Lab}(\mathcal{G})=\{x\}
and
\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{G})\subseteq\mathrm{con}(\mathcal{O}_{1},\dot{\neg}C)\cap\mathrm{con}(D,\mathcal{O}_{2})
. Moreover, as this interpolation sequent is free of (in)equalities, i.e.
\Gamma,\Phi,\Psi=\emptyset
, we note that by the first two properties of Lemma
5
,
\mathcal{G}
must be of the following form:

\mathcal{G}=\{(\ \vdash x:C_{i,1},\ldots,x:C_{i,k_{i}})\ |\ 1\leq i\leq n\}.

By Lemmas
7
–
9
, we have
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}},x:\dot{\neg}C,x:I
and
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{2}},x:D,x:\dot{\neg}I
are provable in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
such that
I=\bigsqcap\bigsqcup\mathcal{G}
. Both of the sequents
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{1}},x:\dot{\neg}C\sqcup I
and
\vdash x:\dot{\neg}\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{O}_{2}},x:\dot{\neg}I\sqcup D
are provable with proofs
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
in
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{O})
by applying
(\sqcup)
, respectively. By Corollary
1
,
\mathcal{O}\vDash C\sqsubseteq I
and
\mathcal{O}\vDash I\sqsubseteq D
.

Last, we argue that the computation of
I
is in
\mathsf{EXPTIME}
relative to the size of
\pi
. Note that applying Lemma
5
is composed of the following steps: first, we partition the sequents in
\pi^{\prime}
by starting with the conclusion of
\pi^{\prime}
and working our way up the proof toward the initial sequents placing active formulae of rule instances within the same component of the partition as their corresponding principal formulae. Second, we assign interpolants to all of the initial sequents and work our way back down toward the conclusion of the proof by computing the interpolant of the conclusion of a rule from the interpolants of its premises, and applying the
(O)
when needed. The only problematic operation is the
(O)
rule as it may exponentially increase the size of interpolants, meaning the calculation of
I
may be exponential in the worst-case.
∎
